that the hypothesis implies that the homomorphism $(f^*(\varOmega^1_{Y/k}))_x\to(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x$ is surjective; hence, replacing $X$ by an open neighbourhood of $x$, we may suppose that the homomorphism $f^*(\varOmega^1_{Y/k})\to\varOmega^1_{X/k}$ is surjective (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg. comm.}, chap. II, § 5, n\textsuperscript{o} 1, prop. 2); consequently $f$ is \emph{unramified} (17.2.2). We will first see that we may restrict to the case where $x$ is \emph{rational over $k$}. Indeed, if we set $k'=\boldsymbol{k}(x)$, and $X'=X\otimes_k k'$, $Y'=Y\otimes_k k'=\operatorname{Spec}(k'[T_1,\ldots,T_n])$, there exists a point $x'\in X'$ above $x$ such that $\boldsymbol{k}(x')=k'$. To prove that $f$ is étale at the point $x$, it suffices to show that $f'=f_{(k')}:X'\to Y'$ is étale at the point $x'$ (17.7.1, (ii)); moreover, $f'$ is unramified (17.3.3, (iii)) and we have $\dim_{x'}(X')=n$ (4.2.7). In the same way, replacing $k'$ by an algebraically closed extension of $k'$, we may suppose that $k$ is \emph{algebraically closed}. Then set $y=f(x)$, $A=\sh{O}_{Y,y}$, $B=\sh{O}_{X,x}$; since the residue field of $B$ is equal to $k$, the same holds for that of $A$, so $x$ (resp. $y$) is a closed point of $X$ (resp. $Y$) (\textbf{I}, 6.4.2), and consequently we have $\dim(A)=\dim(B)=n$. Since $f$ is unramified, the homomorphism $\widehat A\to\widehat B$ is surjective (17.4.4, f'')); but since $\dim(\widehat A)=\dim(\widehat B)=n$, and $\widehat A$, being a regular ring, is integral, the homomorphism $\widehat A\to\widehat B$ is also injective (\textbf{0}, 16.3.10). This homomorphism is therefore bijective, which completes the proof that $f$ is étale at the point $x$ (17.6.3, \emph{e'')}).

\begin{corollary}[17.15.4]
\label{IV.17.15.4}
Under the general hypotheses of (17.15.3), suppose moreover that $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is a finite and separable extension of $k$ and that the germs $(g_i)_x$ belong to $\mathfrak m_x$. Then the conditions a), b), c) of (17.15.3) are also equivalent to each of the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{d)}] The $n$ germs $(g_i)_x$ generate the maximal ideal $\mathfrak m_x$ of $\sh{O}_{X,x}$.
  \item[d')] The ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is regular and the $(g_i)_x$ form a regular system of parameters for this ring \textup{(\textbf{0}, 17.1.6)}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

Indeed, d') trivially implies d). On the other hand, since $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is a finite and separable extension of $k$, we have $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}=0$ (\textbf{0}, 20.6.20), and $\boldsymbol{k}(x)=\sh{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak m_x$ is a formally smooth $k$-algebra for the discrete topologies (\textbf{0}, 19.6.1); hence the exact sequence (\textbf{0}, 20.5.14.1) applies to $A=k$, $B=\sh{O}_{X,x}$, $\mathfrak{K}=\mathfrak m_x$, and yields a canonical isomorphism
\[
\delta:\mathfrak m_x/\mathfrak m_x^2\xrightarrow{\sim}(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x\otimes_{\sh{O}_{X,x}}\boldsymbol{k}(x).
\]
From this we first deduce the equivalence of conditions c) and d), taking into account Nakayama's lemma. On the other hand, if $f$ is étale at the point $x$, the ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is regular and of dimension $n$, since $x$ is a closed point of $X$ (\textbf{I}, 6.4.2), and $n$ elements of $\mathfrak m_x$ that generate this maximal ideal then necessarily form a regular system of parameters for $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ (\textbf{0}, 17.1.6), which proves that a) implies d').

\begin{proposition}[17.15.5]
\label{IV.17.15.5}
Let $k$ be a field, $X$ a prescheme locally of finite type over $k$, $x$ a point of $X$, and $n=\dim_x(X)$. The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{a)}] $X$ is smooth over $k$ at the point $x$.
  \item[\textup{b)}] $X$ is differentially smooth over $k$ at the point $x$.
  \item[\textup{c)}] $(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x$ is a free $\sh{O}_{X,x}$-module of rank $n$.
  \item[\textup{d)}] $(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x$ is an $\sh{O}_{X,x}$-module admitting a system of $n$ generators.
----- English, ega4/ega4-17.tex, page IV-4 101 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{101}

  \item[\textup{e)}] There exist a perfect extension $k'$ of $k$ and an open neighbourhood $U$ of $x$ in $X$ such that the prescheme $U\otimes_k k'$ is regular.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

The fact that $X$ is smooth over $k$ at the point $x$ implies the existence of an open neighbourhood $U$ of $x$ that is smooth over $k$; hence $U\otimes_k k'$ is regular for every extension $k'$ of $k$ (17.15.2), which proves that a) implies e). Conversely, e) implies a), since then $U\otimes_k k'$ is smooth over $k'$ (17.15.2), hence $U$ is smooth over $k$ (17.7.1, (ii)).

We have already proved that a) implies c) (17.10.2); c) implies d) trivially, and the fact that d) implies a) follows from (\textbf{0}, 20.4.7) and (17.15.3, \emph{c)}).

Finally, we have already seen that a) implies b) (17.12.4). Conversely, to prove that b) implies a), we may restrict to the case where $x$ is rational over $k$, by considering, as in the proof of (17.15.3), a point $x'$ of $X'=X\otimes_k k'$ above $x$ such that $\boldsymbol{k}(x')=k'=\boldsymbol{k}(x)$, and using again (17.7.1, (ii)) and the fact that if $X$ is differentially smooth at the point $x$, then $X'$ is differentially smooth at the point $x'$ (16.10.4). Supposing therefore that $x$ is rational over $k$, the fact that $f$ is smooth at $x$ then follows from hypothesis b) and from (17.12.5) applied to the $k$-section $u:\operatorname{Spec}(k)\to X$ such that $u(\operatorname{Spec}(k))=\{x\}$.

\begin{corollary}[17.15.6]
\label{IV.17.15.6}
Let $X$ be a prescheme of finite type over a field $k$. For $X$ to be smooth over $k$, it is necessary and sufficient that the $\sh{O}_X$-module $\varOmega^1_{X/k}$ be locally free, and that the local rings at the maximal points of $X$ be fields that are separable extensions of $k$ (this last condition being automatically satisfied if $k$ is a perfect field and $X$ a reduced prescheme).
\end{corollary}

The conditions are necessary, for if $X$ is smooth over $k$, it follows from (17.10.2) that $\varOmega^1_{X/k}$ is locally free; on the other hand, $X$ is regular, hence \emph{a fortiori} reduced, so at every maximal point $x$ of $X$, $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is a field, which must be a formally smooth $k$-algebra for the discrete topologies (17.5.1), hence a separable extension of $k$ (\textbf{0}, 19.6.1).

The conditions are sufficient. Indeed, we may restrict to the case where $X$ is connected, so that $\varOmega^1_{X/k}$ is locally free of constant rank $n$. For every maximal point $x$ of $X$, we then have $\operatorname{rg}_{\mathbf{k}(x)}\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}=n$ since $\sh{O}_{X,x}=k(x)$; since by hypothesis $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is a separable extension of $k$, we have $\Upsilon_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}=0$ (\textbf{0}, 20.6.3), hence $\deg.\operatorname{tr}_k \boldsymbol{k}(x)=n$ by Cartier's equality (\textbf{0}, 21.7.1). All the irreducible components of $X$ therefore have the same dimension $n$ (5.2.1), and we conclude that $X$ is smooth over $k$ at every point by virtue of the fact that c) implies a) in (17.15.5).

\begin{corollary}[17.15.7]
\label{IV.17.15.7}
If $k$ is of characteristic 0, then, for $X$ to be smooth over $k$ at the point $x$, it is necessary and sufficient that $(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x$ be a free $\sh{O}_{X,x}$-module.
\end{corollary}

This follows from (16.12.2) and from the equivalence of b) and a) in (17.15.5).

\begin{proposition}[17.15.8]
\label{IV.17.15.8}
Let $k$ be a field, $X$ a prescheme locally of finite type over $k$, and $x$ a point of $X$; set $n=\dim_x(X)$, $r=\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})$, so that $n-r=\deg.\operatorname{tr}_k \boldsymbol{k}(x)$ \textup{(5.2.3)}. Let $(g_i)_{1\leq i\leq n}$ be a family of $n$ sections of $\sh{O}_X$ over $X$ such that $(g_i)_x\in\mathfrak m_x$ for $1\leq i\leq r$; let $f:X\to Y=\operatorname{Spec}(k[T_1,\ldots,T_n])$ be the morphism corresponding to the $k$-homomorphism $k[T_1,\ldots,T_n]\to\Gamma(X,\sh{O}_X)$ that sends $T_i$ to $g_i$. The following conditions are equivalent (and imply that $X$ is smooth over $k$ at the point $x$ and that $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is a separable extension of $k$):
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{a)}] $f$ is étale at the point $x$.
----- English, ega4/ega4-17.tex, page IV-4 102 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{102}

  \item[\textup{b)}] The $(g_i)_x$ such that $1\leq i\leq r$ generate $\mathfrak m_x$ (and consequently form a regular system of parameters for $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ \textup{(\textbf{0}, 17.1.6)}), and the images in $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}$ of the elements $(d_{X/k}g_i)_x$ for $r+1\leq i\leq n$ generate $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

Indeed, we have (\textbf{0}, 20.5.12.1) the exact sequence of $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$-modules
\[
\label{IV.17.15.8.1}
\tag{17.15.8.1}
\mathfrak m_x/\mathfrak m_x^2\to(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x\otimes_{\sh{O}_{X,x}}k(x)\to\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}\to0
\]
and condition b) consequently implies that the $(d_{X/k}g_i)_x$ generate $(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x$, taking into account Nakayama's lemma; the fact that b) implies a) therefore follows from (17.15.3). Conversely, if a) holds, the $(d_{X/k}g_i)_x$ for $1\leq i\leq n$ form a basis of $(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x$ by virtue of (17.15.3). If $t_i$ ($1\leq i\leq n$) is the image of $(g_i)_x$ in $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$, we conclude from the above that the $dt_i$ generate $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}$ for $1\leq i\leq n$, and since by hypothesis $t_i=0$ for $1\leq i\leq r$, the $dt_i$ for $r+1\leq i\leq n$ already generate $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}$. Since $\deg.\operatorname{tr}_k \boldsymbol{k}(x)=n\setmin r$, it follows from Cartier's equality (\textbf{0}, 21.7.1) that $\Upsilon_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}=0$, hence $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is a separable extension of $k$ (\textbf{0}, 20.6.3), and the sequence of $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$-vector spaces
\[
\label{IV.17.15.8.2}
\tag{17.15.8.2}
0\to\mathfrak m_x/\mathfrak m_x^2\to(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x\otimes_{\sh{O}_{X,x}}k(x)\to\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}\to0
\]
is exact ((\textbf{0}, 20.5.14) and (\textbf{0}, 19.6.1)); moreover, the $dt_i$ for $r+1\leq i\leq n$ form a basis of $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}$, so none of the $t_i$ such that $r+1\leq i\leq n$ can be zero. This shows that the images of the $(g_i)_x$ in $\mathfrak m_x/\mathfrak m_x^2$ for $1\leq i\leq r$ necessarily generate $\mathfrak m_x/\mathfrak m_x^2$, hence the $(g_i)_x$ for $1\leq i\leq r$ generate $\mathfrak m_x$ by virtue of Nakayama's lemma, which completes the proof that a) implies b).

\begin{corollary}[17.15.9]
\label{IV.17.15.9}
Let $X$ be a prescheme locally of finite type over a field $k$, $x$ a point of $X$, and set $n=\dim_x(X)$, $r=\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})$. The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{a)}] The ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is regular and $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is a separable extension of $k$.
  \item[\textup{b)}] $X$ is smooth over $k$ at the point $x$, and the canonical homomorphism
  \[
  \mathfrak m_x/\mathfrak m_x^2\to(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x\otimes_{\sh{O}_{X,x}}\boldsymbol{k}(x)
  \]
  is injective.
  \item[\textup{c)}] There exist $n$ sections $g_i$ of $\sh{O}_X$ over an open neighbourhood $U$ of $x$ such that $(g_i)_x\in\mathfrak m_x$ for $1\leq i\leq r$ and such that the morphism $U\to\operatorname{Spec}(k[T_1,\ldots,T_n])$ corresponding to the $g_i$ (cf. (17.15.8)) is étale at the point $x$.
  \item[\textup{d)}] There exist $n$ sections $g_i$ \textup{(}$1\leq i\leq n$\textup{)} of $\sh{O}_X$ over an open neighbourhood $U$ of $x$ such that the $(g_i)_x$ for $1\leq i\leq r$ generate $\mathfrak m_x$ and the images in $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}$ of the $(d_{U/k}g_i)_x$ for $r+1\leq i\leq n$ generate $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

The equivalence of c) and d) follows from (17.15.8). Moreover, we have seen in the proof of (17.15.8) that condition \emph{c)} implies that $X$ is smooth over $k$ at the point $x$ and that the sequence (17.15.8.2) is exact; hence c) implies b). Condition b) implies that the ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is regular, so $\mathfrak m_x/\mathfrak m_x^2$ is of rank $r$; moreover, $(\varOmega^1_{X/k})_x$ is then a free $\sh{O}_{X,x}$-module of rank $n$ (17.10.2); since the sequence (17.15.7.2) is exact by hypothesis, $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}$ is of rank $n-r=\deg.\operatorname{tr}_k \boldsymbol{k}(x)$, and Cartier's equality shows that $\Upsilon_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}=0$, so $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is separable over $k$ (\textbf{0}, 20.6.3); thus b) implies a). Finally, if a)

\begin{proposition}[17.15.8]
\label{IV.17.15.8}
Soient $k$ un corps, $X$ un préschéma localement de type fini sur $k$, $x$ un point de $X$; posons $n=\dim_x(X)$, $r=\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})$, de sorte que $n\setmin r=\deg.\operatorname{tr}_k \boldsymbol{k}(x)$ \textup{(5.2.3)}. Soit $(g_i)_{1\leq i\leq n}$ une famille de $n$ sections de $\sh{O}_X$ au-dessus de $X$, telles que $(g_i)_x\in\mathfrak m_x$ pour $1\leq i\leq r$; soit $f:X\to Y=\operatorname{Spec}(k[T_1,\ldots,T

holds, we again deduce from Cartier's equality that $\Omega^1_{\mathbf{k}(x)/k}$ is of rank $n\setmin r$. Since, on the other hand, the ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is regular, the existence of the $g_i$ satisfying the conditions of d) is immediate.

\begin{remarks}[17.15.10]
\label{IV.17.15.10}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] For a prescheme $X$ of finite type over $k$ to be smooth over $k$, it does not suffice that $\varOmega^1_{X/k}$ be locally free, as is shown by the example where $X=\operatorname{Spec}(K)$, $K$ being a finite extension of $k$ that is not separable.
  \item[(ii)] When $k$ is not perfect, it can happen that $X$ is smooth over $k$ without $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ being separable over $k$. We obtain an example by taking $X=\operatorname{Spec}(k[T])$ and for $x$ the point corresponding to the principal prime ideal $(T^p\setmin\lambda)$ ($p>0$ the characteristic of $k$, $\lambda\in k\setmin k^p$).
  \item[(iii)] However, if $X$ is a prescheme smooth over $k$, the set of closed points of $X$ such that $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is separable (and finite) over $k$ is dense in $X$. Indeed, let $f:X\to\operatorname{Spec}(k)$ be the structural morphism; for every $x_0\in X$, there are an open neighbourhood $U$ of $x_0$ in $X$ and a factorization of $f|U:U\xrightarrow{g}\operatorname{Spec}(k[T_1,\ldots,T_n])\xrightarrow{h}\operatorname{Spec}(k)$ where $g$ is étale (17.11.4). Since we may restrict to the case where $k$ is not perfect, hence infinite, the set of points of the open subset $g(U)$ that are rational over $k$ is non-empty; if $y$ is such a point and $x\in U$ a point above $y$, then $x$ is closed in $X$ and $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is separable over $k(y)=k$ (17.6.2).
\end{enumerate}
\end{remarks}

\begin{proposition}[17.15.11]
\label{IV.17.15.11}
Let $X$ be a prescheme of finite type over a field $k$. The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{a)}] $X$ is étale over $k$.
  \item[\textup{b)}] $X$ is unramified over $k$.
  \item[\textup{c)}] $X$ is isomorphic to $\operatorname{Spec}(A)$, where $A$ is a finite and separable $k$-algebra.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

This follows from (17.6.2) and (17.4.2), taking into account the fact that condition b) implies here that $X$ is finite over $k$.

\begin{proposition}[17.15.12]
\label{IV.17.15.12}
Let $k$ be a field and $X$ a prescheme locally of finite type over $k$. For there to exist an everywhere dense open subset $U$ of $X$ that is smooth over $k$, it is necessary and sufficient that for every maximal point $x$ of $X$, $X$ be reduced at this point and $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ be a separable extension of $k$. For there to exist an everywhere dense open subset $U$ of $X$ such that $U_{\mathrm{red}}$ is smooth over $k$, it is necessary and sufficient that for every maximal point $x$ of $X$, $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ be a separable extension of $k$.
\end{proposition}

The second assertion evidently follows from the first. To say that there exists an everywhere dense open subset $U$ smooth over $k$ means that $X$ is smooth over $k$ at each of its maximal points $x$. For this it is necessary that $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ be regular (17.15.1) and \emph{a fortiori} reduced, hence a field, equal to $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$; moreover (17.15.1), $\boldsymbol{k}(x)\otimes_k k'$ must be regular for every radicial extension $k'$ of $k$, so $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ must be a separable extension of $k$ (4.3.5); the converse is immediate, by virtue of (17.15.1).

\begin{corollary}[17.15.13]
\label{IV.17.15.13}
Let $X$ be an algebraic prescheme over a field $k$. Then there exist an everywhere dense open subset $U$ in $X$ and a finite radicial extension $k'$ of $k$ such that $(U_{(k')})_{\mathrm{red}}$ is smooth over $k'$.
\end{corollary}

Indeed, there is such an extension $k'$ for which $(X_{(k')})_{\mathrm{red}}$ is geometrically reduced over $k'$ (4.6.6), which is equivalent to saying (4.6.1) that for every maximal point $x'$ of $X_{(k')}$, $\boldsymbol{k}(x')$ is a separable extension of $k'$. We therefore conclude from (17.15.12) that there is an everywhere dense open subset $U'$ of $X_{(k')}$ such that $U'_{\mathrm{red}}$ is smooth over $k'$. But the

On a en effet (\textbf{0}, 20.5.12.1) la suite exacte de $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$-modules
\[
\label{IV.17.15.8.1}
\tag{17.15.8.1}

\begin{proposition}[17.15.14]
\label{IV.17.15.14}
Let $X$ be an algebraic prescheme over $k$, of dimension $\leq1$. Then there exists a finite radicial extension $k'$ of $k$ such that the normalization \textup{(\textbf{II}, 6.3.8)} of $(X_{(k')})_{\mathrm{red}}$ is smooth over $k'$.
\end{proposition}

Let us first prove the following two lemmas:

\begin{lemma}[17.15.14.1]
\label{IV.17.15.14.1}
Let $X$ be a reduced prescheme whose set of irreducible components is locally finite, and $f:Y\to X$ a morphism. For $Y$ to be $X$-isomorphic to the normalization $X'$ of $X$ \textup{(\textbf{II}, 6.3.8)}, it is necessary and sufficient that the following conditions be satisfied:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] $Y$ is normal;
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] $f$ is an integral and birational morphism.
\end{enumerate}
When there exists in $X$ a dense and normal open subset (which is always the case when $X$ is excellent \textup{(7.8.3)}, in particular when $X$ is locally of finite type over a field), we may replace condition \textup{(ii)} by the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(ii \emph{bis})}] $f$ is integral and there exists a dense open subset $U$ in $X$ such that $f^{-1}(U)$ is dense in $Y$ and the restriction $f^{-1}(U)\to U$ of $f$ is an isomorphism.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

The question being local on $X$, we may suppose that $X$ has only finitely many irreducible components; let $X_i$ ($1\leq i\leq m$) be the reduced sub-preschemes of $X$ having these components as underlying spaces. We know (\textbf{II}, 6.3.6) that $X'$ is the sum of the normalizations $X'_i$ of the $X_i$; each of the structural morphisms $f_i:X'_i\to X_i$ is therefore integral and birational, hence $f$ is integral and birational; it is immediate that (ii) implies (ii \emph{bis}) when there exists a dense and normal open subset $V$ in $X$, by taking $U=V$. Conversely, if conditions (i) and (ii) are fulfilled, $Y$ has only finitely many irreducible components $Y_i$ ($1\leq i\leq m$), $Y_i$ dominating $X_i$ for each index $i$ (6.15.4); $Y$ is the sum of the $Y_i$ since it is normal, and the morphism $g_i:Y_i\to X_i$ through which the restriction $Y_i\to X$ of $f$ factors (\textbf{I}, 5.2.2) is birational; since $X_i$ and $Y_i$ are integral, it then follows from the fact that $g_i$ is integral and $Y_i$ normal that, for every affine open subset $V$ of $X_i$, $\Gamma(g_i^{-1}(V),\sh{O}_{Y_i})$ is the integral closure of $\Gamma(V,\sh{O}_{X_i})$, hence $Y$ is canonically identified with $X'$ (\textbf{II}, 6.3.4). Finally, if we suppose condition (ii \emph{bis}) satisfied, we may restrict to the case where $U$ is the union of disjoint irreducible open subsets $U_i$ ($1\leq i\leq m$), so that $f^{-1}(U)$ is the disjoint union of the $V_i=f^{-1}(U_i)$. Since the $V_i$ are irreducible and $f^{-1}(U)$ is dense in $Y$, the $\overline{V}_i$ are the irreducible components of $Y$, and consequently $f$ is birational.

\begin{lemma}[17.15.14.2]
\label{IV.17.15.14.2}
Let $k$ be a field and $X$ an algebraic prescheme over $k$. Then there exists a finite radicial extension $k'$ of $k$ such that $(X_{(k')})_{\mathrm{red}}$ is geometrically reduced over $k'$ and its normalization is geometrically normal over $k'$.
\end{lemma}

Taking into account (4.6.6) and (\textbf{I}, 5.1.8), we may restrict to the case where $X$ is already geometrically reduced over $k$. Let $p$ be the characteristic exponent of $k$ and $k_1=k^{p^{-\infty}}$ the smallest perfect extension of $k$; $k_1$ is thus the inductive limit of the finite radicial extensions of $k$. Set $X_1=X_{(k_1)}$, which is reduced by hypothesis, and let $Y_1$ be its normalization; if $f_1:Y_1\to X_1$ is the structural morphism, $f_1$ is therefore finite (7.8.3, (vi)) and surjective, and there exists a dense open subset $U_1$ in $X_1$ such that $f_1^{-1}(U_1)=V_1$ is dense in $Y_1$ and 
----- French corrections on page IV-4 103 -----
[id 20654]
OLD: et $k(x)$ est séparable sur $k(y)=k$
NEW: et $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ est séparable sur $\boldsymbol{k}(y)=k$
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): ale} (17.11.4). Comme on peut se borner au cas où $k$ est non parfait, donc infini, l'ensemble des points de l'ouvert $g(U)$ qui sont \emph{rationnels} sur $k$ est non vide; si $y$ est un tel point et $x\in U$ un point au-dessus de $y$, $x$ est fermé dans $X$ et $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ est séparable sur $\boldsymbol{k}(y)=k$ (17.6.2).
\end{enumerate}
\end{remarks}

\begin{proposition}[17.15.11]
\label{IV.17.15.11}
Soit $X$ un préschéma de type fini sur un corps $k$. Les conditions suivantes sont équivalentes:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{a)}] $X$ est étale sur $k$.
  \item[\

NEW: égal à $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$; en outre (17.15.1), $\boldsymbol{k}(x)\otimes_k k'$
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): ère. Dire qu'il existe un ouvert partout dense $U$ lisse sur $k$ signifie que $X$ est lisse sur $k$ en chacun de ses points maximaux $x$. Il est nécessaire pour cela que $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ soit régulier (17.15.1) et \emph{a fortiori} réduit, donc un \emph{corps}, égal à $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$; en outre (17.15.1), $\boldsymbol{k}(x)\otimes_k k'$ doit être régulier pour toute extension radicielle $k'$ de $k$, donc $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ doit être une extension séparable de $k$ (4.3.5); la réciproque est immédiate, en vertu de (17.15.1).

\begin{corollary}[17.15.13]
\label{IV.17.15.13}
Soit $X$ un présché

=================== PAGE IV-4 105 ===================
----- (English page IV-4 104: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, ega4/ega4-17.tex, page IV-4 105 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{105}the morphism $V_1\to U_1$, restriction of $f_1$, is an isomorphism (17.15.14.1). Applying (8.9.1) and (8.10.5, (vi) and (x)), we therefore first see that there exist a finite radicial extension $k''$ of $k$ and a finite surjective morphism $Y''\to X''=X_{(k'')}$ such that $Y_1=Y''\times_{X''}X_1=Y''\otimes_{k''}k_1$; since $Y_1$ is normal and $k_1$ is perfect, it follows from (6.7.7) that $Y''$ is geometrically normal over $k''$. Since the projections $X_1\to X''$ and $Y_1\to Y''$ are integral, surjective and radicial morphisms, they are homeomorphisms (2.4.5), and if $U''$ and $V''$ are the images of $U_1$ and $V_1$ in $X''$ and $Y''$ respectively, these are dense open subsets of $X''$ and $Y''$ respectively such that $V''=f''^{-1}(U'')$, where $f'':Y''\to X''$ is the structural morphism. Since we have $U_1=U''\otimes_{k''}k_1$, it follows from (8.10.5, (i)) that there exists a finite radicial extension $k'$ of $k''$ such that, if we set $X'=X\otimes_k k'=X''\otimes_{k''}k'$, $Y'=Y''\otimes_{k''}k'$, $f'=f''_{(k')}:Y'\to X'$, and if $U'$ and $V'$ are the images of $U''$ and $V''$ in $X'$ and $Y'$ respectively, the restriction $V'\to U'$ of $f'$ is an isomorphism. Since $Y'$ is normal and $f'$ is integral and birational, we conclude from (17.15.14.1) that $Y'$ is isomorphic to the normalization of $X'$, which proves the lemma, since $Y'$ is geometrically normal.

Let us now return to the proof of (17.15.14), and apply Lemma (17.15.14.2) to $k$ and $X$; since $\dim(X)\leq1$, we also have $\dim(X_{(k')})\leq1$ (4.1.4), and the normalization $Y'$ of $X_{(k')}$ is also of dimension $\leq1$ (5.4.2 and \textbf{II}, 7.4.6). To say that $Y'$ is geometrically normal over $k'$ then amounts to saying, by virtue of the definitions and of (\textbf{II}, 7.4.5), that $Y'$ is geometrically regular over $k'$, hence smooth over $k'$ (17.5.1), which proves (17.15.14).

\begin{proposition}[17.15.15]
\label{IV.17.15.15}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a morphism locally of finite presentation. For $f$ to be smooth at a point $x\in X$, it is necessary and sufficient that $f$ be flat at the point $x$ and that $\varOmega^1_f$ be an $\sh{O}_X$-module that is locally free in a neighbourhood of $x$, of rank at $x$ equal to $\dim_x f$.
\end{proposition}

The necessity of the conditions follows from (17.5.1) and (17.10.2). Conversely, if these conditions are satisfied, and if $y=f(x)$, it suffices to show (17.5.1) that $f^{-1}(y)$ is smooth over $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$ at the point $x$; but this follows from the definition of $\dim_x f$ (17.10.1), from (16.4.5) and from (17.15.5).

\subsection{Quasi-sections of flat or smooth morphisms.}
\EGAReaderTarget{ega4.c04.subsec.17-16}{subsection:IV.17.16}

The statements of this subsection supplement those of (14.5), under flatness hypotheses.

\begin{proposition}[17.16.1]
\label{IV.17.16.1}
Let $f:X\to S$ be a flat morphism locally of finite presentation. Let $s\in S$, and let $x$ be a closed point of $X_s$ such that $\sh{O}_{X_s,x}$ is a Cohen--Macaulay ring; then there exist an open neighbourhood $U$ of $s$ in $S$ and a sub-prescheme $X'\subset f^{-1}(U)$ of $X$ such that $x\in X'$ and the morphism $X'\to U$, restriction of $f$, is flat, quasi-finite and of finite presentation.
\end{proposition}

The question being local on $X$ and $Y$, we may suppose $f$ of finite presentation. Let $(t_i)_{1\leq i\leq m}$ be a system of parameters of the local ring $\sh{O}_{X_s,x}$; there exist an affine open neighbourhood $V=\Spec(A)$ of $x$ in $X$ and $m$ sections $g_i$ of $\sh{O}_X$ over $V$ such that
----- English, ega4/ega4-17.tex, page IV-4 106 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{106}

the images of the $(g_i)_x\in\sh{O}_{X,x}$ in $\sh{O}_{X_s,x}=\sh{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak m_s\sh{O}_{X,x}$ are equal to the $t_i$. Let $X'$ be the closed sub-prescheme of $V$ defined by the ideal of $A$ generated by the $g_i$; since the sequence $(t_i)$ is regular by hypothesis (\textbf{0}, 16.5.7), it follows from (11.3.8) that, replacing $V$ if necessary by a smaller open neighbourhood, we may suppose that the morphism $X'\to S$, restriction of $f$, is flat and of finite presentation. On the other hand, since the $t_i$ form a system of parameters of $\sh{O}_{X_s,x}$, the ring $\sh{O}_{X'_s,x}$ is Artinian by definition, and since $x$ is closed in $X'_s$, we conclude that it is isolated in $X'_s$. Replacing $V$ if necessary by a smaller neighbourhood of $x$, we conclude, thanks to (13.1.4), that the morphism $X'\to S$ is quasi-finite.

\begin{corollary}[17.16.2]
\label{IV.17.16.2}
Let $f:X\to S$ be a morphism that is faithfully flat and locally of finite presentation. Then there exists a morphism $g:S'\to S$ that is faithfully flat, locally of finite presentation and locally quasi-finite, such that there exists an $S$-morphism $S'\to X$ (in other words, such that there exists an $S'$-section of $X'=X\times_S S'$ \textup{(\textbf{I}, 3.3.14))}. If $S$ is quasi-compact (resp. quasi-compact and quasi-separated), we may suppose $S'$ affine (resp. $S'$ affine and the morphism $g$ quasi-finite).
\end{corollary}

For every $s\in S$, the fibre $X_s$ is non-empty by hypothesis and is a prescheme locally of finite type over $\boldsymbol{k}(s)$; the set $U_s$ of points $x$ of $X_s$ at which $\sh{O}_{X_s,x}$ is a Cohen--Macaulay ring is open in $X_s$ (6.11.3) and is non-empty, since it contains the maximal points of $X_s$ (\textbf{0}, 16.5.1); consequently it contains a point $x_s$ closed in $X_s$ (10.4.7). Let $X'(s)$ be an affine sub-prescheme of $X$ containing $x_s$ and satisfying the conditions of (17.16.1). To obtain a prescheme $S'$ satisfying the conditions of the statement, it suffices to take the sum of the $X'(s)$, where $s$ runs through $S$. Since the morphism $X'(s)\to S$ is flat and locally of finite presentation, the image $U(s)$ of $X'(s)$ is open in $S$ (2.4.6); when $S$ is quasi-compact, we can therefore cover $S$ by finitely many $U(s_j)$, and the prescheme $S'$, sum of the $X'(s_j)$, still answers the question and is affine. If moreover $S$ is quasi-separated, we may suppose the open immersions $U(s)\to S$ quasi-compact (1.2.7), hence of finite presentation (1.6.2), and then the morphisms $X'(s_j)\to S$ are of finite presentation (1.6.2), and consequently so is the morphism $S'\to S$.

\begin{corollary}[17.16.3]
\label{IV.17.16.3}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] Let $f:X\to S$ be a smooth morphism. Let $s\in S$, and let $x$ be a closed point of $X_s$ such that the residue field $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is separable over $\boldsymbol{k}(s)$; then, in the conclusion of (17.16.1), we can take $X'$ such that the morphism $X'\to U$, restriction of $f$, is étale.
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] Let $f:X\to S$ be a smooth surjective morphism. Then, in the conclusions of \textup{(17.16.2)}, we may moreover suppose that $g:S'\to S$ is étale.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

It is clear that (ii) is deduced from (i) as (17.16.2) was deduced from (17.16.1), taking into account the fact that, for every $s\in S$, since $X_s$ is non-empty and smooth over $\boldsymbol{k}(s)$, there exists a closed point $x\in X_s$ such that $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is separable over $\boldsymbol{k}(s)$ (17.15.10, (iii)). It therefore suffices to prove (i). Note that the ring $\sh{O}_{X_s,x}$ is then regular; if we repeat the construction made in (17.16.1), taking for $(t_i)$ a regular system of parameters of $\sh{O}_{X_s,x}$, the ring $\sh{O}_{X'_s,x}$ is a field isomorphic to $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$, hence separable over $\boldsymbol{k}(s)$ by hypothesis. The conclusion then follows from (17.6.1, \emph{c$'$)}).

\begin{proposition}[17.16.4]
\label{IV.17.16.4}
Let $S$ be a quasi-compact and quasi-separated prescheme, and $f:X\to S$ a surjective morphism locally of finite presentation. Then there exists a finite family $(S_i)_{i\in I}$
----- French corrections on page IV-4 105 -----
[id 20431]
OLD: anneau de Cohen--Macaulay; alors
NEW: anneau de Cohen-Macaulay; alors
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): omplètent ceux de (14.5), moyennant des hypothèses de platitude.

\begin{proposition}[17.16.1]
\label{IV.17.16.1}
Soit $f:X\to S$ un morphisme plat localement de présentation finie. Soient $s\in S$, $x$ un point fermé de $X_s$ tel que $\sh{O}_{X_s,x}$ soit un anneau de Cohen-Macaulay; alors il existe un voisinage ouvert $U$ de $s$ dans $S$ et un sous-préschéma $X'\subset f^{-1}(U)$ de $X$ tel que $x\in X'$ et que le morphisme $X'\to U$, restriction de $f$, soit plat, quasi-fini et de présentation finie.
\end{proposition}

La question étant local

=================== PAGE IV-4 106 ===================
----- (English page IV-4 105: shown above in this file) -----
----- (English page IV-4 106: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, ega4/ega4-17.tex, page IV-4 107 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{107}

of affine sub-preschemes of $S$, of finite presentation over $S$, pairwise disjoint, with union $S$, and having the following property: for each $i\in I$, there exist two finite surjective morphisms of finite presentation
\[
  S_i''\xrightarrow{g_i}S_i'\xrightarrow{h_i}S_i,
\]
where $h_i$ is étale and $g_i$ is flat and radicial, and an $S$-morphism $S_i''\to X$ (in other words, an $S_i''$-section of $X_i''=X\times_S S_i''$).
\end{proposition}

There is a finite covering $(U_i)_{1\leq i\leq n}$ of $S$ by affine open subsets; for every $i$, the intersection
\[
  W_i=U_i\cap\left(\bigcup_{j<i}U_j\right)
\]
is quasi-compact (1.2.7), so there exists a closed sub-prescheme $V_i$ of $U_i$ having $U_i\setmin W_i$ as underlying space and defined by an ideal of finite type of the ring of $U_i$; hence $V_i$ is an affine sub-prescheme of $S$ that is of finite presentation over $S$ (1.6.2). It is clear that we may restrict to proving the proposition with $S$ replaced by $V_i$ and $X$ by $X\times_S V_i$. In other words, we may already suppose $S$ affine. On the other hand, $X$ is the union of affine open subsets $W_\alpha$, and the $f(W_\alpha)$ are constructible in $S$ (1.8.4) and form a covering of $S$; consequently (1.9.9) there exists a finite subfamily $(W_{\alpha_j})_{1\leq j\leq m}$ such that the $f(W_{\alpha_j})$ already form a covering of $S$. Then let $X'$ be the prescheme sum of the sub-preschemes induced on the open subsets $W_{\alpha_j}$ of $X$; it is immediate that it suffices to prove the proposition with $X$ replaced by $X'$, since an $S$-morphism $S_i''\to X'$ gives by composition an $S$-morphism $S_i''\to X'\to X$.

We may therefore suppose $X$ \emph{affine} and $f$ of finite presentation. We can then (8.9.1) write $X$ in the form $X_0\times_{S_0}S$, where $S_0$ is Noetherian and $X_0\to S_0$ is a morphism of finite type, which we may suppose surjective (8.10.5, (vi)). If the proposition is proved for this morphism, it will follow immediately for $X$ by the base change $S\to S_0$. We may therefore suppose moreover that $S$ is Noetherian and $f$ is of finite type.

Let $s_h$ $(1\leq h\leq r)$ be the maximal points of $S$. Let us show that it suffices to prove the statement with $S$ replaced by a sufficiently small open neighbourhood $U_h$ of each of the $s_h$, and $X$ by the inverse image of $U_h$ in $X$. Indeed, suppose the proposition established in this case, and let us argue by Noetherian induction ($\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm I}$, 2.2.2), supposing the statement established for every closed sub-prescheme of $S$ having an underlying space $\neq S$. We may suppose that the $U_h$ pairwise have no point in common; if $T$ is a closed sub-prescheme having $S\setmin(\bigcup_h U_h)$ as underlying space, the induction hypothesis implies that the statement is true for $T$; since it is also true for each of the $U_h$, it is evidently true for $S$.

Since we may evidently (replacing $S$ by $S_{\mathrm{red}}$ and $X$ by $X\times_S S_{\mathrm{red}}$) suppose $S$ reduced, each of the $\sh{O}_{S,s_h}$ is a field $\boldsymbol{k}(s_h)$. Suppose that we have proved the proposition when $S$ is the spectrum of a field and $f$ is of finite type. Then the existence of the $U_h$ follows from the method of (8.1.2, $a$)) and from (8.8.2, (i) and (ii)), (8.10.5, (vi), (vii) and (x)), (11.2.6, (ii)) and (17.7.8, (ii)).

Suppose therefore that $S=\Spec(k)$, where $k$ is a field, $X$ being of finite type over $k$ and $X\neq\varnothing$. Since $X$ is Noetherian, there exists in $X$ a closed point $x$ ($\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm I}$, 2.1.3), so $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is a finite extension of $k$ (1.6.4.2). Consequently there is a finite separable extension $k'$ of $k$ such that $k''=\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is a finite radicial extension of $k'$. We then answer the question by taking $I$ reduced to one element, $S_i'=\Spec(k')$, $S_i''=\Spec(k'')$.

\begin{corollary}[17.16.5]
\label{IV.17.16.5}
Let $f:X\to S$ be a surjective morphism locally of finite presentation. Then there exists a surjective morphism $g:S'\to S$, locally of finite presentation and locally quasi-finite, such that there exists an $S$-morphism $S'\to X$ (in other words, such that there exists an $S'$-section of $X'=X\times_S S'$). If $S$ is quasi-compact (resp. quasi-compact and quasi-separated), we may suppose $S'$ affine (resp. $S'$ affine and the morphism $g$ of finite presentation and quasi-finite).
\end{corollary}

It suffices to prove the corollary supposing $S$ affine: indeed, $S$ is the union of a family $(S_\alpha)$ of affine open subsets, and if for each $\alpha$, $S'_\alpha$ is affine and the morphism $g_\alpha:S'_\alpha\to S_\alpha$ answers the question and is of finite presentation and quasi-finite, then, taking for $S'$ the prescheme sum of the $S'_\alpha$, with $g:S'\to S$ coinciding with $g_\alpha$ on each of the $S'_\alpha$, this morphism answers the question; moreover, if $S$ is quasi-compact, we may suppose the family $(S_\alpha)$ finite, hence $S'$ affine; if in addition $S$ is quasi-separated, the immersions $S_\alpha\to S$ are of finite presentation (1.6.2), hence so is $g$.

Consider therefore the case where $S$ is affine: we then form the finite morphisms of finite presentation $S_i''\to S_i$ of (17.16.4); since the immersions $S_i\to S$ are of finite presentation (the $S_i$ being affine), the morphisms $g_i'':S_i''\to S_i\to S$ are of finite presentation and quasi-finite, and we answer the question by taking $S'$ equal to the sum of the $S_i''$ and $g$ coinciding with $g_i''$ on each of the $S_i''$.

\begin{proposition}[17.16.6]
\label{IV.17.16.6}
Let $S$ be a quasi-compact and quasi-separated prescheme, and $f:X\to S$ a morphism of finite presentation such that, for every $s\in S$, $X_s=f^{-1}(s)$ is a prescheme proper over $\boldsymbol{k}(s)$. Then there exists a finite family $(S_i)_{i\in I}$ of affine sub-preschemes of $S$, of finite presentation over $S$, pairwise disjoint, with union $S$, and such that, for every $i$, the morphism $X_i=X\times_S S_i\to S_i$ is proper and flat. If moreover, for every $s\in S$, $X_s$ is finite over $\boldsymbol{k}(s)$, we can take the $S_i$ such that each of the morphisms $X_i\to S_i$ factors as
\[
  X_i\xrightarrow{u_i}X_i'\xrightarrow{h_i}S_i
\]
where $u_i$ and $h_i$ are finite and locally free \textup{(18.2.7)}, $h_i$ is étale, and $u_i$ is radicial and surjective.
\end{proposition}

We follow a course analogous to that of (17.16.4). We first reduce to the case where $S$ is affine and $f$ is flat, using the generic flatness theorem (8.9.5) (observe that the sub-preschemes $S_i'$ defined in the proof of (8.9.5) are affine). Since $f$ is of finite presentation, we can then write $X=X_0\times_{S_0}S$, where $S_0$ is Noetherian and $X_0\to S_0$ is a flat morphism of finite type (11.2.7); moreover, each fibre $(X_0)_{s_0}$ is again proper over $\boldsymbol{k}(s_0)$, as follows from (2.7.1, (vii)), and we are therefore reduced to the case where $S$ is Noetherian. By Noetherian induction and application of the procedure of (8.1.2, $a$)) (this time also using (8.10.5, (xii))), we are finally reduced, in order to prove the first assertion, to the case where $S$ is the spectrum of a field, which follows trivially from the hypothesis (with $S_i=S$). We treat in the same way the case where $X_s$ is supposed finite over $\boldsymbol{k}(s)$ for every $s\in S$ (this time one must use (2.7.1, (xv)), (8.10.5, (x), (iv), (vi) and (vii)), (17.7.8) and (2.1.12)), and we are reduced to proving the last assertion of the statement when $S=\Spec(k)$ is the spectrum of a field. But then $X=\Spec(A)$, where $A$ is a finite $k$-algebra (\textbf{I}, 6.4.4); so $A$ is the direct product of finite $k$-algebras $A_j$ that are local rings $(1\leq j\leq m)$; since $A_j$ is an Artinian ring and a

\section{Supplement on \'etale morphisms. Henselian local rings and strictly local rings}
\label{section:IV.18}
\egalabel{IV.18}{18}

In the present section we study various properties peculiar to \'etale morphisms.  Moreover, the notion of an \'etale morphism makes it possible to develop, in a very natural way, Nagata's theory of Henselian rings, as well as that of strictly local rings.  These rings play an important role in many recent developments, appearing whenever one needs a process of ``localisation'' finer than that furnished by the Zariski topology (cf.\ for example~[43]), pending the appearance of the chapter of our Treatise devoted to the study of the ``\'etale topology.''

\subsection{A remarkable equivalence of categories.}
\label{subsection:IV.18.1}

\begin{proposition}[18.1.1]
\label{IV.18.1.1}
Let $S$ be a prescheme, $S_0$ a closed subprescheme of $S$, $X_0$ an $S_0$-prescheme smooth (resp.\ \'etale) over $S_0$, and $x_0$ a point of $X_0$.  Then there exist an open neighbourhood $U_0$ of $x_0$ in $X_0$, an $S$-prescheme $U$ smooth (resp.\ \'etale) over $S$, and an $S_0$-isomorphism
\[
 U\times_SS_0\xrightarrow{\sim}U_0.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Observe that if $X_0$ is \'etale over $S_0$ at $x_0$, it is \emph{a fortiori} unramified over $S_0$ at that point.  If an $S$-prescheme $U$ smooth over $S$ has been constructed such that $U\times_SS_0$ is isomorphic to $U_0$, then the fibres of the morphisms $U_0\to S_0$ and $U\to S$ containing $x_0$ are isomorphic.  It follows that $U$ is unramified over $S$ at $x_0$ \sref{IV.17.4.1},~d), hence also in a neighbourhood of $x_0$; replacing $U$ by this neighbourhood, we conclude that $U$ is \'etale over $S$.  It is therefore enough to prove the proposition when $X_0$ is merely supposed smooth over $S_0$.

The question being local on $S$ and on $X_0$, we may suppose that $S=\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ and $X_0=\operatorname{Spec}(C_0)$ are affine, so that $S_0=\operatorname{Spec}(A_0)$, where $A_0$ is a quotient ring of $A$, and
\[
 C_0=B_0/\mathfrak{J}_0,\qquad B_0=A_0[T_1,\ldots,T_n],
\]
where $\mathfrak{J}_0$ is an ideal of finite type in $B_0$; finally, $C_0$ is a formally smooth $A_0$-algebra for the discrete topologies.  Let $\mathfrak p_0$ be the ideal $\mathfrak j_{x_0}$ in $C_0$; then $\mathfrak p_0=\mathfrak q_0/\mathfrak{J}_0$, where $\mathfrak q_0$ is a prime ideal of $B_0$.  The Jacobian criterion ($\mathbf{0}$, 22.6.4), together with ($\mathbf{0}$, 19.1.12), shows that there exist in $\mathfrak{J}_0$ a family of $r$ polynomials $u_i$ $(1\leq i\leq r)$ and indices $j_h$ $(1\leq h\leq r)$ such that the images of the $u_i$ in
\[
 (\mathfrak{J}_0)_{\mathfrak q_0}/(\mathfrak{J}_0^2)_{\mathfrak q_0}
\]
generate this $(B_0)_{\mathfrak q_0}$-module and
\begin{equation}
 \det(\partial u_i/\partial T_{j_h})\notin\mathfrak q_0.
 \tag{18.1.1.1}\label{IV.18.1.1.1}
\end{equation}

Since $(B_0)_{\mathfrak q_0}$ is a local ring, Nakayama's lemma shows that we may suppose that the images of the $u_i$ in $(\mathfrak{J}_0)_{\mathfrak q_0}$ generate this $(B_0)_{\mathfrak q_0}$-module and then, replacing $X_0$ if necessary by an affine open neighbourhood of $x_0$, that the $u_i$ generate $\mathfrak{J}_0$ ($\mathbf{0}_{\mathbf I}$, 5.2.2).  Put $B=A[T_1,\ldots,T_n]$; then $B_0$ is a quotient ring
----- French corrections on page IV-4 108 -----

FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): $ un morphisme de type fini et plat (11.2.7); en outre chaque fibre $(X_0)_{s_0}$ est encore propre sur $\boldsymbol{k}(s_0)$, comme il résulte de (2.7.1, (vii)) et on est donc ramené au cas où $S$ est noethérien. Par récurrence noethérienne et application du procédé de (8.1.2, \emph{a)}) (en utilisant aussi cette fois (8.10.5, (xii))), on est finalement ramené, pour prouver la première assertion, au cas où $S$ est le spectre d'un corps, qui résulte trivialement de l'hypothèse (avec $S_i=S$). On traite de même le cas où $X_s$ est supposé fini

of $B$, $\mathfrak q_0$ is the image of a prime ideal $\mathfrak q$ of $B$, and $\mathfrak q$ is the inverse image of $\mathfrak q_0$.  For every $i$, let $v_i\in B$ be an element whose image in $B_0$ is $u_i$, and let $\mathfrak{J}$ be the ideal of $B$ generated by the $v_i$, so that $\mathfrak{J}_0$ is the image of $\mathfrak{J}$ in $B_0$.

The proposition will be proved by taking for $U$ an open neighbourhood of the point of $\operatorname{Spec}(B/\mathfrak{J})$ corresponding to the prime ideal $\mathfrak p=\mathfrak q/\mathfrak{J}$, provided that we show that $(B/\mathfrak{J})_{\mathfrak q/\mathfrak{J}}$ is a formally smooth $A$-algebra for the discrete topologies.  This follows from the Jacobian criterion: the images of the $v_i$ in $\mathfrak{J}_{\mathfrak q}/\mathfrak{J}_{\mathfrak q}^2$ generate this $B_{\mathfrak q}$-module, and \eqref{IV.18.1.1.1} gives
\[
 \det\left(\frac{\partial v_i}{\partial T_{j_h}}\right)\notin\mathfrak q.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[18.1.2]
\label{IV.18.1.2}
Let $S$ be a prescheme, $S_0$ a closed sub-prescheme of $S$ whose underlying space is identical to that of $S$.
Then the functor
\[
X\mapsto X\times_S S_0
\]
from the category of $S$-preschemes \'etale over $S$, to the category of $S_0$-preschemes \'etale over $S_0$, is an equivalence of categories.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let us first show that this functor is \emph{fully faithful}.
Let $X$, $Y$ be two $S$-preschemes \'etale over $S$, and set $X_0=X\times_S S_0$, $Y_0=Y\times_S S_0$.
If $Z=X\times_S Y$, the set $\operatorname{Hom}_S(X,Y)$ is in canonical bijective correspondence with the set of $X$-sections $\Gamma(Z/X)$, and likewise $\operatorname{Hom}_{S_0}(X_0,Y_0)$ is in canonical bijective correspondence with $\Gamma(Z_0/X_0)$, where $Z_0=Z\times_S S_0=X_0\times_{S_0}Y_0$.
Now $Z$ is \'etale over $X$, $Z_0$ is \'etale over $X_0$, and $X_0$ (resp.\ $Z_0$) is a closed subprescheme of $X$ (resp.\ $Z$) having the same underlying space.  The open subsets of $Z$ on which the restriction of $Z\to X$ is surjective and radicial are therefore the \emph{same} as the open subsets of $Z_0$ having the corresponding properties, and our assertion follows from \sref{IV.17.9.3}.

To complete the proof, it suffices to show that for every $S_0$-prescheme $X_0$ \'etale over $S_0$, there exists an $S$-prescheme $X$ \'etale over $S$ and an $S_0$-isomorphism $X_0\xrightarrow{\sim}X\times_S S_0$.
By virtue of Proposition~\sref{IV.18.1.1}, there exists an open covering $(U_\alpha)$ of $X_0$ and for each $\alpha$, an $S$-prescheme $V_\alpha$ which is \'etale over $S$, and finally an $S_0$-isomorphism $\theta_\alpha:U_\alpha\xrightarrow{\sim}V_\alpha\times_S S_0$.
Furthermore, by virtue of the first part of the proof, the identity of $U_\alpha\cap U_\beta$ lifts uniquely, via $\theta_\alpha$ and $\theta_\beta$, to an $S$-isomorphism $\varphi_{\alpha\beta}$ between the corresponding open subpreschemes of $V_\alpha$ and $V_\beta$, and it is immediate, for the same reason, that these isomorphisms satisfy the glueing condition (\sref[0]{0.4.1.7}).
Hence there exists an $S$-prescheme $X$ such that the $V_\alpha$ identify canonically with sub-preschemes induced on the open subsets of $X$, the $\theta_\alpha$ identify with $S_0$-isomorphisms which coincide on the intersections $U_\alpha\cap U_\beta$, and define an $S_0$-isomorphism $X_0\xrightarrow{\sim}X\times_S S_0$.
It is clear that $X$ is \'etale over $S$ \sref{IV.17.3.2}, which completes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.1.3]
\label{IV.18.1.3}
Let $S$ be a prescheme, $X$ an $S$-prescheme \'etale over $S$, $S'$ an $S$-prescheme, $S_0'$ a closed sub-prescheme of $S'$ having the same underlying space.
Then the canonical map $X(S')_S\to X(S_0')_S$ \sref[I]{I.3.4.3} is bijective.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, set $X'=X\times_S S'$, $X_0'=X\times_S S_0'$, so that $X(S')_S=\Gamma(X'/S')$ and $X(S_0')_S=\Gamma(X_0'/S_0')$;
the corollary follows from the fact that the functor defined in \sref{IV.18.1.2} (with $S$ and $S_0$ replaced by $S'$ and $S_0'$ respectively) is fully faithful (or directly from \sref{IV.17.9.3}).
\end{proof}

\label{subsection:IV.18.2}

Notons que si $X_0$ est étale sur $S_0$ au point $x_0$, il est \emph{a fortiori} non ramifié sur $S_0$ en ce point; si l'on a construit un $S$-préschéma $U$ lisse sur $S$ tel que $U\times_S S_0$ soit isomorphe à $U_0$, comme les fibres des morphismes $U_0\to

\begin{env}[18.2.1]
\label{IV.18.2.1}
Given a ring $A$ and a commutative $A$-algebra $B$ which is \emph{finite} and is a \emph{free} $A$-module, recall (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.}, ch.~VIII, \textsection~12, no.~2) that one defines on $B$ an \emph{$A$-linear form} $\operatorname{Tr}_{B/A}$, the ``trace form''; from it one obtains a \emph{symmetric $A$-bilinear form} (also called the ``trace form'')
\[
\label{IV.18.2.1.1}
(x,y)\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}_{B/A}(xy)
\tag{18.2.1.1}
\]
whose datum is equivalent to that of the associated $A$-linear map $\operatorname{astr}_{B/A}:B\to\check{B}$ from the $A$-module $B$ into its \emph{dual} $\check{B}$; this map is equal to its transpose.  When $A$ is a \emph{field}, saying that this bilinear form is \emph{non-degenerate} is equivalent to saying that $B$ is a \emph{separable} $A$-algebra (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.}, ch.~IX, \textsection~2, prop.~5).

Let $f:A\to A'$ be a ring homomorphism;
set $B'=B\otimes_A A'$, and let $g:B\to B'$ be the canonical homomorphism;
the image under $g$ of a basis of the $A$-module $B$ is then a basis of the $A'$-module $B'$, and it follows from the definitions that, for every $x\in B$,
\[
\label{IV.18.2.1.2}
\operatorname{Tr}_{B'/A'}(g(x))=f(\operatorname{Tr}_{B/A}(x)).
\tag{18.2.1.2}
\]
\end{env}

\begin{env}[18.2.2]
\label{IV.18.2.2}
Consider now a \emph{ringed space} $(X,\sh{O}_X)$ and let $\sh{B}$ be an $\sh{O}_X$-algebra which, as an $\sh{O}_X$-module, is \emph{locally free of finite rank}.  For every open subset $U\subset X$ such that $\sh{B}|_U$ is, as an $\sh{O}_U$-module, isomorphic to $\sh{O}_U^n$ (for some $n$ depending on $U$), $\Gamma(U,\sh{B})$ is a $\Gamma(U,\sh{O}_X)$-algebra which, as a $\Gamma(U,\sh{O}_X)$-module, is free of finite rank.  It therefore defines a $\Gamma(U,\sh{O}_X)$-linear form $\operatorname{Tr}_{\Gamma(U,\sh{B})/\Gamma(U,\sh{O}_X)}$, which we also denote by $\operatorname{Tr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_X,U}$, and hence an associated linear map
\[
\operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_X,U}:\Gamma(U,\sh{B})\to\Gamma(U,\sh{B})^\vee=\Gamma(U,\check{\sh{B}}).
\]

Furthermore, it follows from \sref{IV.18.2.1.2} that these linear maps are compatible with restriction from $U$ to a smaller open subset and therefore define, on the one hand, an \emph{$\sh{O}_X$-module homomorphism}, again called the \emph{trace homomorphism},
\[
\label{IV.18.2.2.1}
\operatorname{Tr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_X}:\sh{B}\to\sh{O}_X
\tag{18.2.2.1}
\]
and, on the other hand, an \emph{$\sh{O}_X$-module homomorphism}
\[
\label{IV.18.2.2.2}
\operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_X}:\sh{B}\to\check{\sh{B}}
\tag{18.2.2.2}
\]
called the \emph{homomorphism associated to the trace}, and equal to its transpose.
It also follows from \sref{IV.18.2.1.2} that for every $x\in X$, we have
\[
\label{IV.18.2.2.3}
(\operatorname{Tr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_X})_x=\operatorname{Tr}_{\sh{B}_x/\sh{O}_{X,x}}
\tag{18.2.2.3}
\]
\[
\label{IV.18.2.2.4}
(\operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_X})_x=\operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}_x/\sh{O}_{X,x}}.
\tag{18.2.2.4}
\]

Finally, under the conditions of \sref{IV.18.2.1}, if we set $X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ and $\sh{B}=\widetilde{B}$, the form $\operatorname{Tr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_X}$ (resp.\ the homomorphism $\operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_X}$) corresponds to the form $\operatorname{Tr}_{B/A}$ (resp.\ the $A$-module homomorphism $\operatorname{astr}_{B/A}$), as also follows from \sref{IV.18.2.1.2}.
\end{env}

\begin{proposition}[18.2.3]
\label{IV.18.2.3}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a finite morphism of preschemes and set $\sh{B}=f_*(\sh{O}_X)$.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{a)}] $f$ is \'etale.
  \item[\textup{a$'$)}] $f$ is a flat morphism of finite presentation, and for every $x\in X$, if $y=f(x)$, the ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak{m}_y\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is a field which is a finite separable extension of $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$.
  \item[\textup{b)}] $\sh{B}$ is a locally free $\sh{O}_Y$-module and, for every $y\in Y$, $\sh{B}_y\otimes_{\sh{O}_{Y,y}}\kappa(y)$ is a finite separable $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$-algebra (hence a direct product of finitely many fields which are finite separable extensions of $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$).
  \item[\textup{c)}] $\sh{B}$ is a locally free $\sh{O}_Y$-module and the homomorphism $\operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_Y}:\sh{B}\to\check{\sh{B}}$ \sref{IV.18.2.2} is bijective.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}

NEW: X\leadsto X\times_S S_0
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): 8.1.1.1) que l'on a $\det(\partial v_i/\partial T_{j_h})\notin\mathfrak q$.

\begin{theorem}[18.1.2]
\label{IV.18.1.2}
Soient $S$ un préschéma, $S_0$ un sous-préschéma fermé de $S$ dont l'espace sous-jacent est identique à celui de $S$. Alors le foncteur
\[
  X\leadsto X\times_S S_0
\]
de la catégorie des $S$-préschémas étales sur $S$, dans la catégorie des $S_0$-préschémas étales sur $S_0$, est une équivalence de catégories.
\end{theorem}

Montrons d'abord que ce foncteur est \emph{pleinement fidèle}. Soient $X$, $Y$ deux $S$-préschémas

----- (English page IV-4 111: shown above in this file) -----

\oldpage[IV-4]{112}
Since $f$ is quasi-compact, the equivalence of $a)$ and $a')$ has already been shown \sref{IV.17.6.2}.
To prove the rest of the proposition, we may restrict to the case where $Y=\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ and $X=\operatorname{Spec}(B)$ are affine, $B$ being a finite $A$-algebra and $\sh{B}=\widetilde{B}$.
To say that $f$ is a morphism of finite presentation is equivalent to saying that $B$ is an $A$-module of finite presentation \sref{IV.1.4.7}.
If, in addition, $f$ is flat, then $B$ is a flat $A$-module; it is known (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, ch.~II, \textsection~5, no.~2, cor.~2 to th.~1) that $B$ is then a \emph{projective} $A$-module.  Thus $\sh{B}$ is a locally free $\sh{O}_Y$-module (\emph{loc.\ cit.}, no.~2, th.~1), and the converse is immediate.
On the other hand, $f^{-1}(y)$ is nothing but the spectrum of the $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$-algebra $\sh{B}(y)=\sh{B}_y/\mathfrak{m}_y\sh{B}_y$, which completes the proof of the equivalence of $a')$ and $b)$.
To see that $b)$ is equivalent to $c)$, note that the second assertion of $b)$ is equivalent to the bijectivity of
\[
 \operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}(y)/\mathbf{k}(y)}:\sh{B}(y)\longrightarrow\sh{B}(y)^\vee.
\]
Since
\[
 \sh{B}(y)=\sh{B}_y/\mathfrak{m}_y\sh{B}_y,
 \qquad
 \sh{B}(y)^\vee=\check{\sh{B}}_y/\mathfrak{m}_y\check{\sh{B}}_y,
\]
and $\sh{B}_y$ and $\check{\sh{B}}_y$ are free $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$-modules, it follows from \sref{IV.18.2.2.4} and Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, ch.~II, \textsection~3, no.~3, cor.\ to prop.~6, that the homomorphism $\operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}_y/\sh{O}_{Y,y}}:\sh{B}_y\to\check{\sh{B}}_y$ is itself bijective;
the converse being evident, this completes the proof.

When an $\sh{O}_X$-Algebra $\sh{B}$ satisfies the equivalent conditions $b)$ and $c)$ of \sref{IV.18.2.3}, we say that $\sh{B}$ is a \emph{finite \'etale $\sh{O}_X$-Algebra}.
When $X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ is affine and $\sh{B}=\widetilde{B}$, this amounts, by virtue of \sref{IV.18.2.3}, to saying that $B$ is a finite \'etale $A$-algebra, or that $B$ is a finite $A$-algebra that is \'etale (in the sense of \sref{IV.17.3.2}).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.2.4]
\label{IV.18.2.4}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a finite morphism of finite presentation, and set $\sh{B}=f_*(\sh{O}_X)$.
Let $y$ be a point of $Y$.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\emph{\alph*)}]
  \item There exists an open neighbourhood $U$ of $y$ in $Y$ such that the restriction $f^{-1}(U)\to U$ of $f$ is an \'etale morphism.
  \item $\sh{B}_y$ is a free $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$-module of finite type and $\sh{B}_y\otimes_{\sh{O}_{Y,y}}\kappa(y)$ is a separable $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$-algebra.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
It is clear that $a)$ implies $b)$ by virtue of \sref{IV.18.2.3}.
On the other hand, $\sh{B}$ is an $\sh{O}_Y$-module of finite presentation \sref{IV.1.6.3} and \sref{IV.1.4.7}; hence, if $\sh{B}_y$ is a free $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$-module, there exists an open neighbourhood $U$ of $y$ in $Y$ such that $\sh{B}|_U$ is a locally free $\sh{O}_U$-module ($\mathbf{0}_{\mathbf I}$, 5.2.7).  Moreover, since by hypothesis the homomorphism $\operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}_y/\sh{O}_{Y,y}}:\sh{B}_y\to\check{\sh{B}}_y$ is bijective, the same result shows that $U$ may be chosen so that

\begin{corollary}[18.2.5]
\label{IV.18.2.5}
Let $Y$ be a quasi-compact or locally Noetherian prescheme, and let $f:X\to Y$ be a finite morphism of finite presentation;
set $\sh{B}=f_*(\sh{O}_X)$.
Suppose that for every \emph{closed} point $y$ of $Y$, $\sh{B}_y$ is a free $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$-module and $\sh{B}_y\otimes_{\sh{O}_{Y,y}}\boldsymbol{k}(y)$ is a separable $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$-algebra.
Then $f$ is \'etale.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, it follows from \sref{IV.18.2.4} that every closed point $y$ of $Y$ has an open neighbourhood $U$ such that the restriction $f^{-1}(U)\to U$ of $f$ is \'etale;
the conclusion follows from the fact that in the two cases considered, every non-empty closed subset of $Y$ contains a closed point (\sref{IV.5.1.11} and \sref[0]{0.2.1.3}).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.2.6]
\label{IV.18.2.6}
If $f:X\to Y$ is a surjective finite \'etale morphism, and if we set $\sh{B}=f_*(\sh{O}_X)$, then the $\sh{O}_Y$-homomorphism $\operatorname{Tr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_Y}:\sh{B}\to\sh{O}_Y$ \sref{IV.18.2.2} (also denoted $\operatorname{Tr}_f$) is surjective.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The question being local, we may, by virtue of \sref{IV.18.2.3}, suppose that $Y=\operatorname{Spec}(A)$, $X=\operatorname{Spec}(B)$, with $B$ a free $A$-module.
The trace pairing, perfect by \sref{IV.18.2.3}, identifies $B$ with $\check{B}$ and sends $1$ to $\operatorname{Tr}_{B/A}$; since $f$ is surjective, $1$ is unimodular, so the trace is surjective.
\end{proof}

\begin{remarks}[18.2.7]
\label{IV.18.2.7}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] When $f:X\to Y$ is a finite morphism such that $f_*(\sh{O}_X)$ is a locally free $\sh{O}_Y$-Module (resp.\ locally free of rank $n$), we say further that $f$ is a \emph{finite locally free} morphism (resp.\ \emph{locally free of rank $n$}).
This condition, by virtue of \sref{IV.18.2.3}, is satisfied if $f$ is a finite \'etale morphism, but does not imply by itself that $f$ is \'etale, as shown by the example where $X=\operatorname{Spec}(K)$ and $Y=\operatorname{Spec}(k)$ are spectra of fields, $K$ being a finite non-separable extension of $k$.
When $f:X\to Y$ is a finite \'etale morphism, we say further that $X$ is an \emph{\'etale covering} of $Y$.
One notes that in this case, $f$ is universally open and universally closed, and in particular $f(X)$ is a subset that is both open and closed of $Y$.

We say that an \'etale covering $X$ of $Y$ is \emph{trivial} if $X$ is a \emph{sum} of a finite number of preschemes \emph{isomorphic} to $Y$.
We say that an \'etale covering $X$ of $Y$ is \emph{locally trivial} if the morphism $f:X\to Y$ is such that for every point $y\in Y$ there is an open neighbourhood $U$ for which the covering $f^{-1}(U)$ of $U$ is trivial.

  \item[(ii)] Let $f:X\to Y$ be a finite morphism, locally free of rank $n$;
set $\sh{B}=f_*(\sh{O}_X)$ and let $u=\operatorname{astr}_{\sh{B}/\sh{O}_Y}:\sh{B}\to\check{\sh{B}}$;
we deduce from this an $n$-th exterior power homomorphism
$\bigwedge^n u:\bigwedge^n\sh{B}\to\bigwedge^n\check{\sh{B}}=(\bigwedge^n\sh{B})^\vee$ of \emph{invertible} $\sh{O}_Y$-Modules, and by \sref[0]{0.5.4.2} an element
\[
\label{IV.18.2.7.1}
d_{X/Y}\in\Gamma(Y,(\bigwedge^n\check{\sh{B}})\otimes_{\sh{O}_Y}(\bigwedge^n\check{\sh{B}}))
\tag{18.2.7.1}
\]
which we call the \emph{discriminant} of $X$ over $Y$.
Furthermore, since $(\bigwedge^n\check{\sh{B}})\otimes_{\sh{O}_Y}(\bigwedge^n\check{\sh{B}})$ is the dual of $(\bigwedge^n\sh{B})\otimes_{\sh{O}_Y}(\bigwedge^n\sh{B})$, $d_{X/Y}$ can also be identified with a homomorphism
\[
\label{IV.18.2.7.2}
(\bigwedge^n\sh{B})\otimes_{\sh{O}_Y}(\bigwedge^n\sh{B})\to\sh{O}_Y
\tag{18.2.7.2}
\]
and we denote by $\mathscr{D}_{X/Y}$ the quasi-coherent ideal of $\sh{O}_Y$ of finite type, \emph{image} of the homomorphism \sref{IV.18.2.7.2}, also called the \emph{discriminant ideal} of $X$ over $Y$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remarks}

D'autre part, $\sh{B}$ est un $\sh{O}_Y$-Module de présentation finie (1.6.3 et 1.4.7), donc, si $\sh{B}_y$ est un $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$-module libre, il existe un voisinage ouvert $U$ de $y$ dans $Y$ tel que $\sh{B}|U$ soit un $\sh{O}_U$-Modul

=================== PAGE IV-4 113 ===================
----- (English page IV-4 112: shown above in this file) -----
----- (English page IV-4 113: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-18.tex, page IV-4 114 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{114}
For the homomorphism $u$ to be bijective, it is necessary and sufficient that $\bigwedge^n u$ be bijective, or again that the section $d_{X/Y}$ have an \emph{invertible} germ at every point $y\in Y$, which is also written $d_{X/Y}(y)\neq 0$ for every $y$ (\sref[0]{0.5.5.2}).
It amounts to the same to say that the discriminant ideal $\mathscr{D}_{X/Y}$ is \emph{equal} to $\sh{O}_Y$.

The terminology ``covering'' introduced in \sref{IV.18.2.7}, (i), is justified by the following proposition:

\begin{proposition}[18.2.8]
\label{IV.18.2.8}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a morphism \'etale, separated and of finite type, and for every $y\in Y$, let $n(y)$ be the geometric number of points of $f^{-1}(y)$.
Then the function $y\leadsto n(y)$ is lower semi-continuous on $Y$.
For it to be continuous at a point $y$ (and hence constant in a neighbourhood of $y$), it is necessary and sufficient that there exists an open neighbourhood $U$ of $y$ such that the restriction $f^{-1}(U)\to U$ of $f$ is a \emph{finite} (\'etale) morphism.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $f$ is quasi-finite \sref{IV.17.6.1} and locally of finite presentation, it amounts to saying that $f$ is finite or that $f$ is proper \sref{IV.8.11.1}:
moreover, each fibre $f^{-1}(y)$ is geometrically reduced over $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$.
The conclusions therefore follow from \sref{IV.15.5.9}, (i) and (ii) and from the fact that $f$ is flat.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.2.9]
\label{IV.18.2.9}
Let $Y$ be a connected prescheme, $f:X\to Y$ a morphism \'etale, separated and of finite type.
For $f$ to be finite (in other words, for $X$ to be an \'etale covering of $Y$), it is necessary and sufficient that all the fibres of $f$ have the same geometric number of points.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remarks}[18.2.10]
\label{IV.18.2.10}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] The example of the ``affine line with a doubled point'' (\sref[I]{I.5.5.11}) shows that a morphism \'etale of finite type of Noetherian preschemes may not be separated;
for this example, the first assertion of \sref{IV.18.2.8} is no longer exact.

  \item[(ii)] For a separated morphism of finite type and \'etale $f:X\to Y$ to make $X$ a \emph{locally trivial} covering, it is necessary and sufficient that for every $x\in X$ there exist an open neighbourhood $U$ of $y=f(x)$ and a $U$-section $g$ of $f^{-1}(U)$ such that $g(y)=x$.
Indeed, the condition is obviously necessary;
the fact that it is also sufficient follows from the fact that every fibre $f^{-1}(y)$ is finite (\sref{IV.17.6.1}) and \sref[I]{I.6.2.2}, from the characterisation of sections of a $Y$-scheme \'etale \sref{IV.17.9.3} and from Proposition~\sref{IV.18.2.8}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remarks}


\subsection{Finite algebras and \'etale algebras.}
\label{subsection:IV.18.3}

\begin{proposition}[18.3.1]
\label{IV.18.3.1}
Let $A$ be a ring, $B$ an $A$-algebra of finite presentation.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] For $B$ to be an unramified $A$-algebra, it is necessary and sufficient that $B$ be a \emph{projective} $(B\otimes_A B)$-module.
  \item[(ii)] Suppose moreover that $B$ is a finite $A$-algebra.  For $B$ to be an \'etale $A$-algebra, it is necessary and sufficient that $B$ be a projective $A$-module and a projective $(B\otimes_A B)$-module.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}

The $(B\otimes_A B)$-module structure on $B$ is that coming from the canonical ring homomorphism $B\otimes_A B\to B$, which is surjective and has kernel $\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}$ (\sref[0]{0.20.4.1}).

(i) To say that the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(B)\to\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ is locally of finite presentation is equivalent to saying that $B$ is an $A$-algebra of finite presentation \sref{IV.1.4.6}.  To say that $B$ is an unramified $A$-algebra means \sref{IV.17.4.2} that
\[
 \operatorname{Spec}\bigl((B\otimes_A B)/\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}\bigr)
\]
is the subprescheme induced on an open and closed subset of $\operatorname{Spec}(B\otimes_A B)$.  This holds if and only if $\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}$ is a \emph{direct-factor ideal} of $B\otimes_A B$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, ch.~II, \textsection~4, no.~3, prop.~15);
but this amounts to saying that the $(B\otimes_A B)$-module quotient $(B\otimes_A B)/\mathfrak{J}_{B/A}$ is projective (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.}, ch.~II, $3^{\text{e}}$ \'ed., \textsection~2, no.~2, prop.~4).

(ii) Recall that a flat $A$-module of finite presentation is projective, and conversely (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, ch.~II, \textsection~5, no.~2, cor.~2 du th.~1), so the assertion (ii) follows from that of (i) and from \sref{IV.17.6.2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[18.3.2]
\label{IV.18.3.2}
Let $A$ be a ring, $\mathfrak{J}$ an ideal of $A$ such that, for the $\mathfrak{J}$-preadic topology, $A$ is separated and complete;
set $A_0=A/\mathfrak{J}$.
Then the functor
\[
B\mapsto B\otimes_A A_0
\]
is an equivalence of the category of finite \'etale $A$-algebras, to the category of finite \'etale $A_0$-algebras.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We will first prove the following lemma:

\begin{lemma}[18.3.2.1]
\label{IV.18.3.2.1}
Let $A$ be a ring, $\mathfrak{J}$ an ideal of $A$ such that, for the $\mathfrak{J}$-preadic topology, $A$ is separated and complete.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] Every projective $A$-module of finite type $M$ is separated and complete for the $\mathfrak{J}$-preadic topology, and hence is the projective limit of the projective $(A/\mathfrak{J}^{n+1})$-modules $M/\mathfrak{J}^{n+1}M$.
  \item[(ii)] Conversely, set $A_n=A/\mathfrak{J}^{n+1}$, and let $(M_n)$ be a projective system of $A_n$-modules such that, for every $n$, the homomorphism $M_{n+1}\otimes_{A_{n+1}}A_n\to M_n$ induced by the transition homomorphism $M_{n+1}\to M_n$ is bijective.
Suppose moreover that the $M_n$ are projective and $M_0$ of finite type.
Then $M=\varprojlim M_n$ is a projective $A$-module of finite type such that the canonical homomorphism $M\otimes_A A_0\to M_0$ is bijective.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) There is a free $A$-module of finite type $L$ such that $M$ is isomorphic to a direct factor of $L$;
as $L$ is separated for the $\mathfrak{J}$-preadic topology, so is every sub-module $N$ of $L$ since $\mathfrak{J}^{n+1}N\subset\mathfrak{J}^{n+1}L$;
in particular $M$ is separated for this topology, and since the surjective homomorphism $f:L\to M$ is continuous for the $\mathfrak{J}$-preadic topology, its kernel $N$ is closed for the topology induced by that of $L$;
since $L$ is complete and $f$ a strict morphism, we conclude that $M$ is complete (Bourbaki, \emph{Top.\ g\'en.}, ch.~IX, $2^{\text{e}}$ \'ed., \textsection~3, no.~1, prop.~4).

(ii) It follows from Nakayama's lemma that, if $M_0$ is generated by a finite family $(x_{i0})$ of $r$ elements and if, for every $n\geq 1$, $x_{in}$ is an element of $M_n$ whose image in $M_{n-1}$ is $x_{i,n-1}$, then $(x_{in})$ $(1\leq i\leq r)$ is a system of generators of $M_n$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, ch.~II, \textsection~3, cor.~2 to prop.~4).  For every $n$, put $L_n=A_n^r$.  If $(e_{in})_{1\leq i\leq r}$ is the canonical basis of $L_n$, let $u_n:L_n\to M_n$ be the $A_n$-linear map such that $u_n(e_{in})=x_{in}$ for every $i$.
By hypothesis, we have a split exact sequence
\[
0\to N_n\xrightarrow{v_n} L_n\xrightarrow{u_n} M_n\to 0
\]
----- French corrections on page IV-4 114 -----
[id 20814]
OLD: est finie (17.6.1)
NEW: est finie ((17.6.1)
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): il suffit que pour tout $x\in X$ il existe un voisinage ouvert $U$ de $y=f(x)$ et une $U$-section $g$ de $f^{-1}(U)$ telle que $g(y)=x$. En effet, la condition est évidemment nécessaire; le fait qu'elle est suffisante résulte de ce que toute fibre $f^{-1}(y)$ est finie ((17.6.1) et (\textbf{I}, 6.2.2)), de la caractérisation des sections d'un $Y$-schéma étale (17.9.3) et de la prop. (18.2.8).
\end{enumerate}
\end{remarks}

\subsection{Algèbres finies et étales.}
\EGAReaderTarget{ega4.c04.subsec.18-3}{subsection:IV.18.3}

\begin{proposition}[18.3.1]
\label{IV.18.3.1}
Soient $A$ un

=================== PAGE IV-4 115 ===================
----- (English page IV-4 114: shown above in this file) -----
----- (English page IV-4 115: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-18.tex, page IV-4 116 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{116}
and since $L_n=L_{n+1}/\mathfrak{J}^{n+1}L_{n+1}$ and $M_n=M_{n+1}/\mathfrak{J}^{n+1}M_{n+1}$, the vertical arrows in the commutative diagram
\[
\xymatrix{
0\ar[r] & N_{n+1}\ar[r]^{v_{n+1}}\ar[d] & L_{n+1}\ar[r]^{u_{n+1}}\ar[d] & M_{n+1}\ar[r]\ar[d] & 0\\
0\ar[r] & N_n\ar[r]_{v_n} & L_n\ar[r]_{u_n} & M_n\ar[r] & 0
}
\]
are all three surjective.
Now we have $M=\varprojlim M_n$ and $L=A^r=\varprojlim L_n$;
setting $N=\varprojlim N_n$, $u=\varprojlim u_n$, $v=\varprojlim v_n$, by (EGA $\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm{III}}$, 13.2.2) the exact sequence
\[
\label{IV.18.3.2.2}
0\to N\xrightarrow{v} L\xrightarrow{u} M\to 0
\tag{18.3.2.2}
\]
is exact.
Moreover, since for every $n$, $v_n$ is left-invertible and $M_n$ is a projective $A_n$-module, by virtue of (\sref[0]{0.19.1.8}) the exact sequence \sref{IV.18.3.2.2} is split, which proves the lemma.
\end{proof}

This being established, let us first show that the functor of \sref{IV.18.3.2} is \emph{fully faithful}.
Set, as in the lemma, $A_n=A/\mathfrak{J}^{n+1}$;
let $B$, $C$ be two finite \'etale $A$-algebras, and set, for every $n$, $B_n=B\otimes_A A_n$, $C_n=C\otimes_A A_n$;
by virtue of \sref{IV.18.3.1} and \sref{IV.18.3.2.1}, $B$ and $C$ are separated and complete for the $\mathfrak{J}$-preadic topology, and we have $B=\varprojlim B_n$, $C=\varprojlim C_n$;
moreover, every $A$-algebra homomorphism $u:B\to C$ is continuous for the $\mathfrak{J}$-preadic topologies, and hence gives a projective system of $A_n$-algebra homomorphisms $u_n=u\otimes 1:B_n\to C_n$, of which it is the projective limit;
the converse being evident, we have a canonical bijection
\[
\operatorname{Hom}_{A\text{-}\mathrm{alg.}}(B,C)\xrightarrow{\sim}\varprojlim\operatorname{Hom}_{A_n\text{-}\mathrm{alg.}}(B_n,C_n).
\]
But since $B$ and $C$ are finite \'etale $A$-algebras, it follows also from \sref{IV.18.1.2} that the canonical map
\[
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{n+1}\text{-}\mathrm{alg.}}(B_{n+1},C_{n+1})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{A_n\text{-}\mathrm{alg.}}(B_n,C_n)
\]
is \emph{bijective} for $n\geq 0$, which proves that the canonical map $\operatorname{Hom}_{A\text{-}\mathrm{alg.}}(B,C)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{A_0\text{-}\mathrm{alg.}}(B_0,C_0)$ is bijective.

To complete the proof of \sref{IV.18.3.2}, it suffices to show that for every finite \'etale $A_0$-algebra $B_0$, there exists a finite \'etale $A$-algebra $B$ and an $A_0$-isomorphism $B_0\xrightarrow{\sim}B\otimes_A A_0$.
Now, by \sref{IV.18.1.2}, there is a projective system $(B_n)$ such that $B_n$ is an \'etale $A_n$-algebra and that the homomorphisms $B_{n+1}\otimes_{A_{n+1}}A_n\to B_n$ are bijective.
It follows from \sref{IV.18.3.1} and \sref{IV.18.3.2.1} that the $A$-algebra $B=\varprojlim B_n$ is a projective $A$-module of finite type and that $B_0$ is isomorphic to $B\otimes_A A_0$.
To prove that $B$ is a finite \'etale $A$-algebra, it suffices, by \sref{IV.18.2.5}, to show that for every maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}$ of $A$, $B_\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}B_\mathfrak{m}$ is a separable $(A/\mathfrak{m})$-algebra.
Now, since $\mathfrak{J}$ is contained in the radical of $A$ (\sref[0]{0.7.1.10}),

\begin{env}[18.3.3]
\label{IV.18.3.3}
\emph{Example.} ---
Proposition \sref{IV.18.3.2} applies in particular when $A$ is a \emph{local} ring, separated and complete, $\mathfrak{J}$ being the maximal ideal of $A$, so that $A_0$ is a field and the finite \'etale $A_0$-algebras are precisely the finite separable $A_0$-algebras, hence the direct products of finite separable field extensions of $A_0$.
In particular, if the field $A_0$ is \emph{separably closed}, these extensions are all identical to $A_0$, and hence every \'etale covering of $\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ is \emph{trivial} \sref{IV.18.2.7} by virtue of \sref{IV.18.3.2}.
\end{env}

\begin{theorem}[18.3.4]
\label{IV.18.3.4}
Let $A$ be a Noetherian ring, $\mathfrak{J}$ an ideal of $A$ such that $A$ is separated and complete for the $\mathfrak{J}$-preadic topology, $A_0=A/\mathfrak{J}$.
Let $S=\operatorname{Spec}(A)$, $S_0=\operatorname{Spec}(A_0)$.
Let $X$ be a proper $S$-scheme, and set $X_0=X\times_S S_0$.
Then the functor
\[
Z\mapsto Z\times_X X_0
\]
from the category of finite \'etale $X$-schemes over $X$, to the category of finite \'etale $X_0$-schemes over $X_0$, is an equivalence of categories.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let us first show that this functor is \emph{fully faithful}.
Let $Z'$ and $Z''$ be two finite \'etale $X$-schemes over $X$.
Set $S_n=\operatorname{Spec}(A/\mathfrak{J}^{n+1})$, $X_n=X\times_S S_n$, $Z'_n=Z'\times_S S_n$, $Z''_n=Z''\times_S S_n$.
It follows from (\sref[III]{III.5.4.1}) that we have a canonical bijection $\operatorname{Hom}_X(Z',Z'')\xrightarrow{\sim}\varprojlim\operatorname{Hom}_{X_n}(Z'_n,Z''_n)$.
Now, by virtue of \sref{IV.18.1.2}, the canonical map $\operatorname{Hom}_{X_{n+1}}(Z'_{n+1},Z''_{n+1})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{X_n}(Z'_n,Z''_n)$ is bijective, which proves our assertion.

It remains to show that if $\sh{B}_0$ is a finite \'etale $\sh{O}_{X_0}$-algebra, there exists a finite \'etale $\sh{O}_X$-algebra $\sh{B}$ and an isomorphism $\sh{B}_0\xrightarrow{\sim}\sh{B}\otimes_{\sh{O}_X}\sh{O}_{X_0}$.
It follows from \sref{IV.18.1.2} that there is a projective system $(\sh{B}_n)$, where $\sh{B}_n$ is a finite \'etale $\sh{O}_{X_n}$-algebra, and the second comparison theorem (\sref[III]{III.5.1.4}) proves the existence of a coherent $\sh{O}_X$-module $\sh{B}$ and of a projective system of isomorphisms $\sh{B}_n\xrightarrow{\sim}\sh{B}\otimes_{\sh{O}_X}\sh{O}_{X_n}$.
The datum of an algebra structure on an $\sh{O}_X$-module $\sh{F}$ is equivalent to that of a unit homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to\sh{F}$ and a multiplication homomorphism $\sh{F}\otimes_{\sh{O}_X}\sh{F}\to\sh{F}$ making certain diagrams involving only tensor powers of $\sh{F}$ commute;
by \sref[III]{III.5.1.3}, \sref[I]{I.10.11.4} and \sref[I]{I.10.11.7}, $\sh{B}$ is therefore endowed in a natural way with a structure of $\sh{O}_X$-algebra for which the isomorphism $\sh{B}_0\xrightarrow{\sim}\sh{B}\otimes_{\sh{O}_X}\sh{O}_{X_0}$ is an isomorphism of algebras.
Moreover, $\sh{B}$ is a \emph{locally free} $\sh{O}_X$-module;
this also follows from \sref[III]{III.5.1.3}, \sref[I]{I.10.11.4}, \sref[I]{I.10.11.7} and from the fact that, in the category of coherent $\sh{O}_X$-modules, the locally free $\sh{O}_X$-modules $\sh{F}$ can be characterized as those for which the functors $\sh{G}\leadsto\shHom_{\sh{O}_X}(\sh{F},\sh{G})$ are \emph{exact}.
Finally, to see that $\sh{B}$ is a finite \'etale $\sh{O}_X$-Algebra, it suffices \sref{IV.18.2.5} to show that for every \emph{closed} point $x\in X$, $\sh{B}_x\otimes_{\sh{O}_{X,x}}\boldsymbol{k}(x)$ is a separable $\boldsymbol{k}(x)$-algebra.
But since the structural morphism $f:X\to S$ is \emph{proper}, $f(x)$ is a closed point of $S$, so it belongs to $S_0$, since $\mathfrak{J}$ is contained in the radical of $A$ (\sref[0]{0.7.1.10});
the conclusion therefore follows from the fact that $X_0=f^{-1}(S_0)$ and that $\sh{B}_0$ is a finite \'etale $\sh{O}_{X_0}$-algebra.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[18.4.1]
\label{IV.18.4.1}
Let $A$ be a ring, $B$ a finite monogenic $A$-algebra, $u$ a generator of the $A$-algebra $B$, $F\in A[\mathrm{T}]$ a polynomial such that $F(u)=0$, $F'$ the derivative polynomial;
set $u'=F'(u)$.
Then the annihilator ideal of $\Omega^1_{B/A}$ in $B$ contains $u'B$;
it is equal to $u'B$ if the ideal $\mathfrak{J}$ of polynomials $G\in A[\mathrm{T}]$ such that $G(u)=0$ is generated by $F$, in other words if the canonical surjection $\varphi:A[\mathrm{T}]/F A[\mathrm{T}]\to B$, which sends the class of $\mathrm{T}$ to $u$, is bijective.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Set $C=A[\mathrm{T}]$, so that $B=C/\mathfrak{J}$.
We have the exact sequence (\sref[0]{0.20.5.12.1})
\[
\mathfrak{J}/\mathfrak{J}^2\to\Omega^1_{C/A}\otimes_C B\to\Omega^1_{B/A}\to 0
\]
and $\Omega^1_{B/A}$ therefore identifies with the quotient $B/\mathfrak{J}'$, where $\mathfrak{J}'$ is the ideal generated by the elements $G'(u)$, where $G$ ranges over a system of generators of the ideal $\mathfrak{J}$ (\sref[0]{0.20.5.13});
whence the lemma immediately follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[18.4.2]
\label{IV.18.4.2}
Let the notation be those of \sref{IV.18.4.1}, let $\mathfrak{q}$ be a prime ideal of $B$.
Then:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] If $\mathfrak{q}$ does not contain $u'$, $B_\mathfrak{q}$ is a formally unramified $A_\mathfrak{p}$-algebra ($\mathfrak{p}$ being the inverse image of $\mathfrak{q}$ in $A$);
in other terms, $\operatorname{Spec}(B_{u'})$ is formally unramified over $\operatorname{Spec}(A)$.
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] Suppose moreover that $F$ is monic and generates $\mathfrak{J}$.
Then, for $\operatorname{Spec}(B)$ to be \'etale over $\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ at the point $\mathfrak{q}$, it is necessary and sufficient that $u'\notin\mathfrak{q}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The hypothesis that $u'\notin\mathfrak{q}$ implies that $\Omega^1_{B_\mathfrak{q}/A_\mathfrak{p}}=0$ (\sref[0]{0.20.5.9}), hence (i) follows from \sref{IV.17.2.1}.
Moreover, under the hypotheses of (ii), $B$ is a free $A$-module by virtue of the Euclidean division;
as the annihilator $\mathfrak{J}'$ of $\Omega^1_{B/A}$ is then equal to $u'B$ by virtue of \sref{IV.18.4.1} and $\Omega^1_{B/A}$ is a $B$-module of finite presentation \sref{IV.16.4.22}, the annihilator of $\Omega^1_{B_\mathfrak{q}/A_\mathfrak{p}}$ is equal to $u'B_\mathfrak{q}$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, ch.~II, \textsection~2, no.~4, formule (g)), and (ii) follows from (i) and from the implication $\textit{c)}\Rightarrow\textit{a)}$ in \sref{IV.17.6.1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.4.3]
\label{IV.18.4.3}
With the notation of \sref{IV.18.4.2}, suppose that $F$ is monic and generates $\mathfrak{J}$.
Then, for $B$ to be an \'etale $A$-algebra, it is necessary and sufficient that $u'$ be invertible in $B$ (or, what amounts to the same, that the ideal of $A[\mathrm{T}]$ generated by $F$ and $F'$ be equal to $A[\mathrm{T}]$).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In view of \sref{IV.18.4.2},~(ii), saying that $\operatorname{Spec}(B)$ is \'etale over $\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ means that $u'$ does not belong to any prime ideal of $B$, i.e.\ that it is invertible in $B$.
\end{proof}

We say that a monic polynomial $F\in A[\mathrm{T}]$ such that the ideal of $A[\mathrm{T}]$ generated by $F$ and $F'$ is equal to $A[\mathrm{T}]$ is \emph{separable};
it is immediate that this definition coincides with the usual definition (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.}, ch.~V, \textsection~7, no.~6) when $A$ is a field.

Moreover, if $k$ is the

\begin{theorem}[18.3.4]
\label{IV.18.3.4}
Soient $A$ un anneau noethérien, $\mathfrak J$ un idéal de $A$ tel que $A$ soit séparé et complet pour la topologie $\mathfrak J$-préadique, $A_0=A/\mathfrak J$. Posons $S=\Spec(A)$, $S_0=\Spec(A_0)$. Soit $X$ un $S$-schéma propre sur $S$, et posons $X_0=X\times_S S_0$. Alors le foncteur
\[
  Z\leadsto Z\times_X X_0
\]
de la catégorie des $X$-schémas finis et étales sur $X$ dans la catégorie des $X_0$-schémas finis e
